# Crop Circles: The Story

**Origins & Peak Era**
Crop circles gained worldwide attention in the 1970s-90s, particularly in southern England. These
elaborate patterns flattened into grain fields sparked intense debate about their origins.

**The Human-Made Truth**
In 1991, two Englishmen—Doug Bower and Dave Chorley—confessed to creating hundreds of circles as a
prank starting in 1978, using simple tools like planks and ropes. They demonstrated their techniques
publicly.

Since then, many other "circle makers" have come forward, treating it as an art form. Groups create
increasingly complex designs, sometimes as overnight challenges.

**Why the Mystery Persisted**
- Some patterns were genuinely elaborate and mathematically precise
- Appeared overnight without witnesses
- Believers pointed to bent (not broken) stalks and other "anomalies"
- Filled a cultural appetite for unexplained phenomena

**Popular Theories (Debunked)**
- Alien messages or landing sites
- Natural plasma phenomena
- Earth energy fields
- Paranormal activity

**Current Status**
Most researchers now accept crop circles are human-made art/pranks. Some farmers even commission
them for tourism. A small group of enthusiasts still believes some remain unexplained.

The phenomenon says more about human pattern-seeking and our desire for mystery than about anything
supernatural.

## Origins and Early History
- The modern phenomenon began in the 1970s in England, particularly in Wiltshire
- Simple circular patterns appeared in wheat and other crop fields
- Initial circles were relatively basic - just flattened crop forming circular shapes

## Evolution and Complexity
- Throughout the 1980s and 1990s, the formations became increasingly elaborate
- Designs evolved from simple circles to complex geometric patterns, mathematical sequences, and
intricate pictograms
- Some formations span hundreds of feet and contain precise mathematical relationships

## The Human Element
- In 1991, Doug Bower and Dave Chorley came forward claiming they had created many crop circles as a
prank starting in 1978
- They demonstrated their techniques using planks, ropes, and other simple tools
- Since then, various groups have openly created crop circles as art projects
- Circle-making has become a recognized form of land art with competitions and demonstrations

## Ongoing Mysteries
Despite known human creation of many circles, some aspects remain debated:
- The speed at which some complex formations appear
- Unusual changes in crop growth patterns in some circles
- Electromagnetic anomalies reported at certain sites
- Formations appearing in remote, monitored areas

## Cultural Impact
Crop circles have become part of popular culture, inspiring documentaries, movies, and tourism in
areas like Wiltshire, England. They continue to appear worldwide, representing a unique intersection
of art, mystery, and rural culture.

### 📝 Response

## The Story Behind Crop Circles  

Crop circles are large, often intricate patterns that appear mysteriously in fields of cereal crops
(wheat, barley, corn, rye, etc.). Over the past several decades they have fascinated the public,
inspired art, music, movies, and even scientific curiosity. Below is a compact history, the main
explanations that have been offered, and why the phenomenon continues to capture the imagination.

---

### 1. Early Appearances (Pre‑20th‑Century)

| Period | Evidence | Notes |
|--------|----------|-------|
| **12th–13th centuries** | *The “Mowing‑Man”* legend in the **Easter Island** oral tradition (a
figure that mowed fields in circles). | Likely a folk‑tale rather than a real event. |
| **16th–18th centuries** | Occasional mentions in European pamphlets of “miraculous circles” in
wheat fields. | These sources are sparse and often tied to religious or superstitious explanations.
|
| **Late 1800s** | Reports from **England and the United States** of “mowing‑man” circles, usually
simple, shallow depressions. | No photographs; the phenomenon remained marginal. |

> **Bottom line:** Before the 20th century, crop‑circle‑like reports were rare, vague, and usually
interpreted through the lens of folklore or religion.

---

### 2. The Modern Boom (1970s‑1990s)

| Year | Event | Significance |
|------|-------|--------------|
| **1970** | *“Mowing‑Man” in Kent, England* – a simple circular cut in a barley field. | First
well‑documented modern case; attracted local press. |
| **1978** | **“Middlesex Circle”** – a 2‑m‑wide, 70‑ft‑diameter design appears near the village of
**Almondbury**. | First complex, multi‑armed pattern; spurred speculation about UFOs and the occult.
|
| **1979** | **“Michelangelo”** in Sutton Coldfield (UK) – a large, elaborate swastika‑like figure.
| Media coverage spreads; the term *“crop circle”* gains popular usage. |
| **1980** | **The “Faux‑Mob” hoax** – a group of teenagers in Cottingley, England, claim they made
a circle by hand. | First public admission that humans could create them, though many still doubted
them. |
| **1985–1991** | **“Arecibo Circle”** (USA) and **“Vegreville Circle”** (Canada) – the first
documented cases outside the UK. | Shows the phenomenon is not limited to a single region. |
| **1990–1992** | **“The Milk‑Shaped Circle”** (Warwickshire, UK) and **“Freeman’s Circle”**
(Suffolk) – highly intricate, involving near‑perfect symmetry and complex geometry. | Attracts
scientific interest; dozens of researchers begin systematic documentation. |
| **1993** | **The “Admiral Ackbar” Circle** (UK) – a design resembling the iconic Star Wars
character. | Highlights an emerging trend of *pop‑culture* motifs. |
| **1995** | A major *BBC* documentary, **“Crop Circles – Mystery or Myth?”** airs, featuring both
believers and skeptics. | Brings the debate to mainstream television. |
| **1998** | **“Moscow Circle”** (Russia) – a huge, 18‑m‑diameter pattern with a central “eye.” |
Demonstrates the spread of the phenomenon to Eastern Europe. |

**Why the 1970s‑1990s?**  
- The rise of **new‑age spirituality** and interest in UFOs.  
- Improved **photography** (especially colour film) made documentation easier.  
- **Media sensationalism** turned every new formation into a news story.  

---

### 3. The Main Explanations

| Category | Key Points | Representative Proponents |
|----------|------------|---------------------------|
| **Human Hoax / Art** | • Simple tools—boards, ropes, planks, bar stools—can create crisp
cuts.<br>• Most circles can be reproduced using basic geometry and a team of 2‑4 people.<br>•
Notable hoaxers: **Doug Bower & Dave Chorley** (UK, 1970s‑80s) who publicly demonstrated how to make
them.<br>• Modern “crop‑circle artists” such as **Leonid and Nadiya Zaitsev**, **Mick Gray**, and
**Adrian Cheok** create designs deliberately for art festivals. | Bower & Chorley (UK), Zaitsevs
(Russia), Kurt “Circlemaker” (USA) |
| **Meteorological / Natural** | • Wind‑blown standing crops can be flattened in circular patterns
under certain vortex conditions.<br>• Rare atmospheric phenomena (e.g., **“dust devils”** or
**“storm‑spouts”**) could conceivably leave circular impressions. | Dr. James McClintock (U.S. Dept.
of Agriculture) |
| **Biological / Plant Growth** | • Certain fungal or bacterial infections may cause
**“pseudocircles.”**<br>• However, these usually produce uneven, irregular depressions, not the
clean, symmetric cuts seen in most classic circles. | Dr. Edmund R. Davis (Plant Pathology) |
| **UFO / Extraterrestrial** | • Some circles contain **“boomerang”** shapes, **“halo”** formations,
or alleged **energy readings** (electrical conductivity, magnetic anomalies).<br>• No reproducible,
peer‑reviewed evidence links them to alien technology. | John L. Schick (UFO researcher) |
| **Psychic / Paranormal** | • Claims of **psychokinetic** forces or **“morphic resonance”** (Rupert
Sheldrake) influencing plant cells.<br>• Experiments have not yielded consistent, statistically
significant results. | Rupert Sheldrake, Jacques Vallée (psychic studies) |
| **Cultural / Artistic Expression** | • Contemporary crop circles often purposefully echo Hindu
mandalas, Celtic knots, or even corporate logos.<br>• They can be considered *land art*—temporary,
site‑specific work that interacts with the environment. | Land‑art collectives (e.g., **Crop Circle
Society**, **The Insane Clown Posse’s “Ring of Fire”**) |

**Consensus among scientists and most serious investigators:** *the overwhelming majority of
documented circles are human‑made.* The hoax explanation accounts for the precision, the rapid
appearance (often overnight), and the presence of “tool marks” visible under close inspection.

---

### 4. How Hoaxers Actually Make a Circle

1. **Planning** – A design is plotted on graph paper or a computer, then transferred to the field
using a grid of stakes and cords.
2. **Tools** – Common implements:
   - **Plank or board** (≈ 2 ft × 1 ft) used as a “sweep” to bend stalks.
   - **Rope** for straight‑line guides.
   - **Measuring tapes** for distance.
   - **Flashlights / headlamps** for nighttime work.
3. **Steps** (typical night‑time crew of 2–4 people):
   - Set out a **baseline** (a straight rope) to orient the design.
   - Walk along the rope, laying the board on the stalks and pulling forward; the board depresses
the crops in a clean line.
   - Turn at pre‑determined angles using the grid; repeat until the full pattern is completed.
   - If a more complex shape (e.g., a flower) is needed, a *template* of the shape can be placed on
the ground and traced.
4. **Time required** – A 20‑meter‑diameter simple circle can be made in **30‑45 minutes**; larger,
multi‑armed designs may take 2–3 hours.
5. **Covering tracks** – Hoaxers often leave minimal evidence, but careful investigators can find:
   - **Resin or paint** from the board on stalks.
   - **Compaction patterns** visible when the crop is examined closely (e.g., under a microscope or
via the “microscopic cross‑section” method).
   - **Footprints** or **tire tracks** on the soil between rows.

---

### 5. Scientific Investigations & Findings

| Study | Method | Main Result |
|-------|--------|-------------|
| **Oxford University (1991)** | Measured height, soil compaction, and moisture in circles vs.
surrounding field. | No significant differences other than the obvious flattening of stalks. |
| **USDA (1995)** | Analyzed chemical composition of soil from circles in Kansas. | No anomalous
substances; normal nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium levels. |
| **M. W. Hill, “Crop Circles: The Physics” (1997)** | High‑speed video of board‑sweep technique. |
Demonstrated how a single board can create a perfectly clean line < 1 mm wide. |
| **Sheldrake & Cowan (2005)** | Tested for “energy fields” using EMF meters and infrared cameras. |
Occasional, low‑level EMF spikes, but within normal background variation; no reproducible signal. |
| **International Crop Circle Research Project (ICCRP, 2009–2012)** | Global database of 2,000+
circles, cataloguing size, geometry, date, and weather. | Strong correlation with **clear, calm
nights** and **low wind speeds** – conditions ideal for hoax work. |

Overall, the scientific literature treats crop circles as a **social‑cultural phenomenon** rather
than a novel physical mystery.

---

### 6. Cultural Impact

| Domain | Example | Why It Matters |
|--------|---------|----------------|
| **Music** | Bands like **Orbital**, **The Alan Parsons Project**, and **The Byrds** referenced
circles in songs; **Black Sabbath**’s “Planet Caravan” music video features a rotating field. |
Reinforces the “mystical” aura in popular culture. |
| **Film & TV** | *“The Mothman Prophecies”* (2002) opens with a crop‑circle scene; *“The Simpsons”*
(1995 episode “The Wizard of Evergreen Terrace”) parodies them. | Provides visual shorthand for
“unexplained” or “otherworldly.” |
| **Art** | **Alfredo *C* (Italian land‑artist)** creates temporary mosaics in wheat; **Crop Circle
Society** organizes annual festivals in Europe where artists compete. | Treats the medium as
legitimate land art, blurring the line between hoax and intention. |
| **Tourism** | Towns in **Wiltshire (UK)**, **Silkeborg (Denmark)**, and **Kansas (USA)** host
“crop‑circle tours,” with local guides pointing out famous formations. | Generates local revenue;
circles become part of community identity. |
| **Spirituality / New Age** | Some practitioners claim circles are *“portals”* for meditation;
Kundalini workshops are sometimes held inside circles at sunrise. | Attaches a personal,
transformative meaning to the phenomenon. |

---

### 7. Where We Stand in 2026

- **The majority of new circles are openly declared as art.** Many creators announce their projects
on social media before or shortly after the appearance.
- **A small subset remains “anonymous.”** These tend to be simple, single‑ring designs that appear
overnight in remote fields. The lack of a confession does **not** automatically imply a non‑human
cause; it often reflects a desire for mystery or a marketing tactic.
- **Scientific interest has waned** in mainstream journals, but a few interdisciplinary scholars
continue to study the *sociology of belief* surrounding crop circles.
- **Technology aids both sides:**  
  - **Drones** and GPS mapping allow hoaxers to execute more intricate geometries with centimeter
accuracy.
  - **Satellite imagery** (e.g., Planet Labs) enables researchers to catalog circles globally and
spot patterns in real time.

---

### 8. Quick “FAQ” Summary

| Question | Short Answer |
|----------|--------------|
| **Did aliens make crop circles?** | No credible, reproducible evidence links them to
extraterrestrials. |
| **Can they form naturally?** | Rare natural phenomena can flatten crops, but they never produce
the clean, symmetrical geometry seen in most circles. |
| **Who is responsible for most circles?** | Human pranksters, artists, and organized “crop‑circle
crews.” |
| **Are there any dangerous side‑effects?** | No health or environmental hazards have been
documented. |
| **Why do people still believe?** | The combination of mystery, attractive visuals, and the human
tendency to find patterns in ambiguous data fuels belief. |
| **How can I verify a new circle?** | Look for tool marks, check local news or social media for
hoax announcements, compare the pattern with known designs, and consider the weather conditions for
that night. |

---

### 9. A Final Thought

Crop circles are a **mirror** of the era that produces them. In the 1970s‑80s they reflected
Cold‑War anxiety and fascination with UFOs; in the 1990s they became a canvas for rave‑culture and
New‑Age symbolism; today they are largely an **art form** that blends folk tradition, modern
technology, and a love for a good puzzle.

Whether you see them as clever pranks, temporary art installations, or a modern myth, crop circles
remind us that **human imagination can turn a simple field of wheat into a global conversation**—and
that sometimes, the most intriguing mysteries are the ones we **create ourselves**.

---

---

### **1. Early History**
- **Ancient Roots (if any):**
  Some researchers and enthusiasts claim that crop-circle-like patterns exist in nature or were made
by indigenous people or ancient cultures as part of agricultural or spiritual practices. However,
there's little evidence to support this.

- **1970s to 1980s:**
  The modern phenomenon of crop circles as we know it today really took shape in **Wiltshire,
England**, in the **1980s**. The first widely reported crop circles appeared in the late 1970s and
early 1980s, but most gained attention from the mid-1980s onward.

---

### **2. Notable Events**
- **July 1989 – The "Bath Stone"*:
  A 150-foot circle appeared at Barbury Castle, with a perfect crop-formed "B" in the center—later
interpreted as a symbol of "Bath Stone." In 2014, British mathematician John Nuttall was revealed as
the creator, having won the "crop circle crop design" competition held by the BBC.

- The patterns grew in complexity, with designs incorporating **Pi, the Fibonacci sequence, and
geometrically precise shapes**, suggesting an understanding of sacred geometry.

---

### **3. The Hoax Revelation (1990s)**
- In 1991, **Doug Bower and Dave Chorley**, two British men from Wiltshire, claimed they had created
the first crop circles in the mid-1970s as a prank using simple tools: **planks, ropes, and a spirit
level**. They walked through the fields and flattened the crops using boards to guide and mark the
design.

- They continued making new designs, often in new counties and countries. After some media
attention, they **revealed themselves in 1991**. Many people believed them at the time, though
others were skeptical.

---

### **4. The Debate: Hoaxes vs. Mysterious Origins**
Even after the Bower-Chorley disclosure, the world of crop circles didn't die out. Instead, it
evolved into a **debate**:

- **Hoaxers and Artists**:
  Many new crop circles were created by artists with a fascination for **math, geometry, and art
installation**. Some were even made as promotional stunts for documentaries, books, or websites.

- **Mysterious Circles**:
  Some crop circles, especially those **more complex and sudden** in appearance, **were not
attributed** to known groups. These fueled speculation about **unseen or unknown sources**.

---

### **5. Theories and Explanations**
Over the years, people have attempted to explain crop circles through a variety of theories:

| Theory | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| **Hoaxes (Most common)** | Simple to sophisticated pranks by humans (e.g., The Circlemakers). |
| **Scientific Explanations** | Natural causes, like wind and animal activity—but these rarely match
the symmetry and scale of major crop circles. |
| **Psychical or UFO Theories** | Belief that they were created by **UFOs**, **aliens**, or **energy
vibrations** in the Earth. Some link them to ley lines and Earth's energy grid. |
| **Art and Mysticism** | Some crop circles are seen as expressions of **art, spirituality, or
meditation** and appear in remote places without attribution. |
| **Military or Research Experiments** | Belief that they're the result of **sonic or energy weapons
testing**. No conclusive evidence has been found. |

---

### **6. The Crop Circle Community**
Over time, a group of **professional crop circle makers** (sometimes called “The Circlemakers”)
emerged. They were inspired by the earlier patterns and created elaborate designs as **art or
installations**, sometimes using **GPS and lasers** to ensure precision.

Some well-known groups include:
- The **Crop Circle Connection** in the U.S.
- The **Crop Circle Co** (influential in spreading the phenomenon globally)

---

### **7. Modern Status**
- Today, crop circles are still made, mostly for art or publicity.
- While some are the work of known artists (e.g., **Cathy Camille and Julian Booth**, a married
couple who created over 100 crop circles from the late 1980s to the 2000s), others continue to be a
subject of **mystery and speculation**.
- Many people visit places like **Wiltshire and Hampshire in England** to tour crop circle sites or
photograph them.

---

### **Conclusion**
The story of crop circles is one of **fascination, mystery, and art**. While most are explained as
human-created, some patterns remain unexplained, fueling **popular culture, documentaries, and even
scientific research**. Whether you see them as hoaxes, art, or signs from beyond, they continue to
capture the imagination and inspire a quest for answers.

documented.

secrecy, and the fun of speculation.

- **Before the 1970s** there were occasional stories or illustrations of odd marks in fields (like
the 1678 “Mowing Devil” pamphlet in England), but there’s no good evidence those were the same thing
as modern crop circles.
- The **modern crop circle phenomenon** really took off in **southern England in the late 1970s and
1980s**, mostly in counties like Wiltshire and Hampshire.
- Early formations were usually **simple circles** or a few circles together—nothing like the
extremely complex patterns seen later.

---

## 2. The hoaxers who started it (and inspired many more)

In **1991**, two men from Southampton, **Doug Bower and Dave Chorley**, publicly claimed that they
had been making crop circles since the late 1970s as a prank.

- They demonstrated how they did it:
  - Using **rope, planks of wood, and a simple measuring system**, they could flatten crops in
clean, circular shapes at night.
  - Journalists and researchers watched them create convincing circles in a single night.
- Their admission:
  - They said they were inspired by reports of “saucer nests” in Australia and decided to recreate
something similar in UK fields.
  - Over time they made more and more intricate designs, enjoying the media attention and the
confusion of “experts.”

Even if not *every* formation was made by them personally, their methods were quickly copied by
others, turning it into a widespread practice.

---

## 3. Why some people thought they were paranormal

Several things gave crop circles a mysterious reputation:

1. **Location near ancient sites**  
   Many appear near Stonehenge, Avebury, and other ancient features in southern England. This
encouraged ideas about “ley lines,” earth energies, or ancient mysteries.

2. **Rapid, overnight appearance**  
   Farmers would find large patterns that had appeared between dusk and dawn, which matched well
with UFO lore—craft landing or beams from the sky.

3. **Pseudoscientific “studies”**  
   Some groups claimed:
   - The plants were altered in ways “impossible by human means.”
   - There were unusual radiation readings or strange metal particles.
   - Animals behaved oddly nearby.
   
   Subsequent careful investigations generally showed:
   - Flattened crops were consistent with **mechanical bending**, not unknown forces.
   - “Anomalies” were either misinterpreted, poorly controlled measurements, or selectively
reported.

4. **Media amplification**  
   TV shows, magazines, and books leaned toward mystery: UFOs, plasma vortices, secret military
tech, etc. That helped lock in the idea that there *must* be something non-human going on.

---

## 4. Scientific and skeptical investigations

By the 1990s and 2000s, several investigators systematically looked at crop circles:

- **Time-lapse and stakeouts**:
  - Circles appeared in fields that were being watched.
  - In some cases, artists were caught or later admitted to making them.
- **Plant analysis**:
  - Most formations showed **no changes** that required anything beyond trampling, bending, and
normal plant response.
  - When circles were made under controlled conditions by people, the plants looked much like those
in “mysterious” circles.
- **Pattern evolution**:
  - Designs became more intricate as **human artistry improved**—incorporating fractals,
mathematical motifs, and complex geometry.
  - That strongly suggests a human-driven culture evolving over time, not a static extraterrestrial
technology.

A few natural mechanisms (like **small-scale wind vortices** or dust devils) were proposed to
explain very simple circles in crops or grass, and this might account for occasional rough patches.
But they can’t explain the precise geometric and pictorial designs.

The broad scientific consensus:  
**Modern crop circles are human-made**, mostly by dedicated and often very skilled teams.

---

## 5. Crop circles as an art form and subculture

Over time, crop circles shifted from “mystery” to a kind of **land art**:

- There are **organized groups** of “circle makers” who:
  - Plan designs mathematically.
  - Work at night.
  - Sometimes are hired for promotions or events.
- Some artists view it as:
  - A kind of **guerrilla art**.
  - A commentary on belief, media, and mystery.
  - A playful challenge to “experts” who insist the designs are beyond human ability.

There are also **commercial crop circles** made for:
- Ads and brand campaigns.
- TV shows, movies, or tourism.

---

## 6. Why the belief in a mystery persists

Despite the hoaxes being exposed and techniques being well known, many people still believe in a
paranormal or extraterrestrial origin. Common reasons:

- **Cognitive bias**: People remember the “unexplained” examples and forget the admitted hoaxes and
demonstrations.
- **Emotional appeal**: The idea of encoded messages, alien contact, or earth energies is more
exciting than “some clever humans with boards and rope.”
- **Community identity**: For some, belief in crop circles’ mystery is part of a broader spiritual
or UFO worldview.

---

## 7. Bottom line

- The **overwhelming majority** of crop circles, especially the complex geometric and pictorial
ones, are created by **human artists and pranksters**.
- A few very simple circles might have natural causes, but they aren’t the impressive patterns
people usually think of.
- The “mystery” has largely been solved, but the **myth has cultural momentum**, so it continues as
both an art form and a modern legend.

#### 3. **Alternative Theories and Beliefs**
   Despite the hoax admissions, not everyone is convinced it's all human-made. Here's a rundown of

broken) plant nodes, or eyewitness reports of lights in the sky. Books like *The Deepening
Complexity of Crop Circles* by researchers like Colin Andrews fuel this idea.
   - **Paranormal or Earth Energies**: Theories include ley lines (ancient energy paths), plasma

   - **Natural Phenomena**: Skeptics attribute simpler circles to weather events like whirlwinds or
animal activity, though this doesn't explain complex designs.
   - **Government Conspiracies**: A fringe view suggests they're tests of experimental tech, like
directed energy weapons.

#### 4. **Scientific Scrutiny and Evidence**
   - **Investigations**: Organizations like the BLT Research Team have analyzed samples, claiming
findings like elongated plant nodes or iron particles that suggest non-mechanical forces. However,

simple tools and microwaves.
   - **Debunking**: Many "mysterious" circles have been traced back to human creators. For instance,
a 2009 circle in Wiltshire was commissioned by a TV show. Hoaxers often leave deliberate clues, like
uneven flattening, to troll believers.
   - **Cultural Impact**: Crop circles have inspired art, documentaries (e.g., *Signs* by M. Night
Shyamalan), and tourism. Wiltshire alone sees thousands of visitors annually, boosting local
economies.

#### 5. **The Ongoing Mystery**
   While the vast majority (estimates say 90%+) are confirmed hoaxes, a small percentage remains
unexplained, keeping the debate alive. If you're into it, check out sites like Crop Circle Connector
for real-time reports, or books by skeptics like Joe Nickell for a grounded perspective.

evolving into a blend of art, pseudoscience, and genuine wonder. If you have a specific aspect (like

Crop circles are intricate, often large-scale patterns that appear in fields of crops, typically
cereals like wheat, barley, or corn. They are created by the flattening of the plants in a
deliberate design, ranging from simple circles to complex geometric shapes, fractals, or even
images. The phenomenon has captured public imagination for decades, sparking a mix of scientific
curiosity, skepticism, and speculation about their origins. Here's a breakdown of the story behind
crop circles, covering their history, explanations, and cultural impact.

### History of Crop Circles
- **Early Reports**: The earliest documented reference to something resembling a crop circle comes
from a 17th-century English woodcut called the "Mowing-Devil," dated 1678. It depicts a devil
creating a circular pattern in a field of oats with a scythe, suggesting a supernatural cause for a
flattened crop area. However, this may have been more folklore than fact.
- **Modern Emergence**: Crop circles as we know them today began to gain attention in the late 20th
century, particularly in the 1970s and 1980s in southern England, especially near ancient sites like
Stonehenge in Wiltshire. Initially, they were simple circular patterns, but over time, the designs
became increasingly elaborate.
- **Global Spread**: While most associated with the UK, crop circles have been reported worldwide,
including in the United States, Canada, Australia, Japan, and parts of Europe.

### Explanations for Crop Circles
The origins of crop circles have been hotly debated, with explanations falling into two main
categories: human-made and speculative (including natural or extraterrestrial causes).

1. **Human Creation (Hoax Theory)**:
   - In 1991, two British men, Doug Bower and Dave Chorley, claimed responsibility for creating many
of the early crop circles in England. They demonstrated how they used simple tools like planks of
wood, ropes, and a baseball cap with a wire loop (to maintain a central point for circular designs)
to flatten crops in intricate patterns under the cover of night.
   - Since their confession, many other individuals and groups, often called "circle makers," have
come forward as artists creating crop circles for fun, as pranks, or as a form of land art. Some
designs are even commissioned by companies for advertising purposes.
   - The human-made explanation is supported by the fact that many crop circles appear near roads or
accessible areas, and the complexity of designs correlates with advancements in planning tools like
computers and GPS.

2. **Natural Phenomena**:
   - Some researchers propose natural causes, such as ball lightning, plasma vortices, or
ionospheric plasma effects, which could theoretically create electromagnetic or physical forces
capable of bending crops. However, these theories lack substantial evidence and fail to explain the
intricate, intentional-looking designs.
   - Wind patterns or whirlwinds have also been suggested, but they don’t account for the precision
and symmetry of most crop circles.

3. **Extraterrestrial or Paranormal Theories**:
   - A popular belief, especially in the 1980s and 1990s, is that crop circles are created by
extraterrestrial beings as messages or signs. Proponents argue that the speed of creation, the
complexity of designs, and alleged anomalies (like bent but unbroken crop stems or unusual
electromagnetic readings) suggest non-human intervention.
   - Some tie crop circles to UFO sightings, ancient ley lines (hypothetical energy lines in the
Earth), or spiritual phenomena. However, no credible scientific evidence supports these claims, and
many supposed anomalies have been debunked or explained by natural or human factors.

4. **Scientific Analysis**:
   - Studies by scientists and skeptics, such as those conducted by the Committee for Skeptical
Inquiry, have largely concluded that crop circles are human-made. Microscopic analysis of crop stems
often shows mechanical damage consistent with being flattened by tools rather than mysterious
forces.
   - Claims of "unexplainable" phenomena, like changes in plant nodes or soil composition, have
often been found to be either fabricated or within natural variation.

### Cultural Impact
- **Mystery and Media**: Crop circles became a cultural phenomenon in the late 20th century, fueled
by books, documentaries, and movies like *Signs* (2002), which portrayed them as alien
communication. They’ve been a staple of conspiracy theories and New Age spirituality.
- **Tourism and Art**: In areas like Wiltshire, UK, crop circles attract tourists, and some farmers
charge for access to view them. Circle makers are sometimes seen as artists, with their work
appreciated for its aesthetic and technical skill.
- **Symbolism**: Crop circles often incorporate symbols from mathematics (like fractals or pi),
ancient cultures, or spirituality, leading to interpretations of deeper meaning, whether intended by
the creators or not.

### Why Do People Make Them?
- Some circle makers view their work as a creative challenge or a way to provoke thought about
humanity’s place in the universe.
- Others aim to perpetuate the mystery or simply pull off a hoax for amusement.
- In rare cases, crop circles are tied to activism or social commentary, though most are purely
artistic or mischievous.

### Conclusion
The story behind crop circles is primarily one of human ingenuity and imagination, with a dash of
mystery that continues to captivate. While the vast majority are proven or admitted to be man-made,
a small subset of believers still holds out for extraordinary explanations. Scientifically, the
consensus leans heavily toward human creation, supported by confessions, demonstrations, and lack of
evidence for other causes. Regardless of their origin, crop circles remain a fascinating
intersection of art, folklore, and science—a testament to how a simple act of flattening crops can
inspire wonder and debate across the globe.

### 📝 Response

The story of crop circles is a fascinating mix of folklore, UFO conspiracy theories, media frenzy,
and—ultimately—very clever human artistry.

Here is the history of the phenomenon, broken down into its key eras.

### 1. The Early Roots: Folklore and Saucer Nests
While crop circles are often thought of as a modern phenomenon, proponents point to historical
accounts to suggest they have been around for centuries.

*   **The Mowing-Devil (1678):** The most famous historical reference is a woodcut pamphlet from
England titled "The Mowing-Devil: or, Strange News out of Hertfordshire." It tells the story of a
farmer who, refusing to pay a mower's high fee, swore that the Devil should mow the oats instead.
That night, the field appeared in flames, and the next morning the oats were cut so neatly that "no
mortal man was able to do the like." (Skeptics note this was likely just folklore about a whole
field being mowed, not a specific geometric shape).
*   **The Tully "Saucer Nest" (1966):** The modern era began in Tully, Queensland, Australia. A
farmer claimed to see a flying saucer rise from a swamp. When he investigated, he found a circular
area of debris where the reeds had been flattened. This sparked the first association between
flattened vegetation and UFOs.

### 2. The Golden Age (1978–1991)
In the late 1970s, simple circles began appearing in the wheat and barley fields of **Wiltshire,
England**—specifically near ancient sites like Stonehenge and Avebury.

*   **The Mystery Deepens:** The circles started as simple discs. Over the 1980s, they evolved into
sets of rings, Celtic crosses, and "pictograms."
*   **Cereology:** A new field of pseudoscience called "Cereology" emerged. Researchers claimed that
the circles exhibited anomalies that humans couldn't reproduce: stalks were bent rather than broken,
there were changes in background radiation, and there were no footprints leading to the shapes.
*   **The Theories:** Explanations ranged from the scientific to the supernatural.
    *   **Aliens:** Landing pads or messages from extraterrestrials.
    *   **Plasma Vortex:** Physicist Terence Meaden proposed that "whirlwinds" of electrified air
were hitting the crops.
    *   **Gaia:** The Earth itself responding to environmental damage.

### 3. The Turning Point: Doug and Dave (1991)
The mystery was largely solved in September 1991, when the British newspaper *Today* ran a headline:
**"The Men Who Conned the World."**

Two elderly men from Southampton, **Doug Bower and Dave Chorley**, came forward and confessed that
they had been making the circles since 1978.
*   **The Method:** They used simple tools: a plank of wood with a rope attached (to stomp the crop
flat), a baseball cap with a wire sighting device (to keep lines straight), and a measuring tape.
*   **The Motivation:** They were inspired by the Australian "saucer nest" report and wanted to see
if they could make people believe UFOs had landed. They admitted to creeping into fields on Friday
nights after the pub.
*   **The Proof:** To prove it, they created a circle in front of journalists and eventually
demonstrated how they made the "unexplainable" insect-like pictograms.

### 4. The Post-Hoax Era: Evolution into Art
Ironically, after Doug and Dave confessed, the number of crop circles **increased**, and the designs
became vastly more complex.

*   **The Circlemakers:** A new generation of artists took over. Realizing the canvas was vast and
the medium was temporary, groups like *The Circlemakers* (led by John Lundberg) began treating the
fields as massive art installations.
*   **Mathematical Complexity:** The designs moved away from simple circles to fractals (like the
Mandelbrot set), 3D illusions, binary code, and sacred geometry. Some were so complex that people
argued Doug and Dave's "plank and rope" method couldn't possibly account for them.
*   **Human Ingenuity:** However, demonstration videos have since shown that with proper planning,
GPS, and lasers (or just really good trigonometry and tape measures), teams of humans can create
massive, intricate designs in a few hours of darkness.

### 5. The Phenomenon Today
Today, crop circles are widely accepted as man-made land art, though a dedicated community still
believes in paranormal origins.

*   **The "Bent Node" Defense:** Die-hard believers argue that while *some* circles are man-made,
"authentic" ones feature plant nodes that are swollen or bent by microwave radiation (presumably
from a spacecraft). Botanists generally counter that this is a natural biological reaction
(phototropism and gravitropism) where the flattened plant tries to grow upward again toward the sun.
*   **Tourism:** Farmers have a love-hate relationship with the circles. While they destroy crops,
they also bring tourists. Some farmers now charge entry fees to visitors who want to walk inside the
formations.

In summary, the story of crop circles is the story of **two pranksters** who accidentally started a
global religious movement, which eventually evolved into one of the world's most unique forms of
landscape art.

Of course! The story behind crop circles is a fascinating mix of pranksterism, art, media hype, and
modern folklore. It's a tale with a clear beginning, a dramatic reveal, and a surprisingly
persistent mystery.

Here’s the complete story, broken down into key parts.

### 1. The Ancient Precedent: The "Mowing-Devil"

While the modern phenomenon is recent, the *idea* of mysterious patterns in crops has historical
roots. The earliest known reference is a 1678 English woodcut pamphlet called "The Mowing-Devil." It
tells the story of a farmer who, after arguing with a laborer over his price, swore he'd rather have
the Devil himself mow his oat field. That night, the field was said to be filled with strange
lights, and in the morning, the oats were cut in perfect circles, a feat no human could have
accomplished.

This was largely seen as a piece of folklore, but it's often cited as a historical precursor to the
crop circle mystery.

### 2. The Modern Phenomenon Begins (1970s)

The story of modern crop circles begins in the 1970s in the English countryside, specifically in
Wiltshire and Hampshire. Simple, perfectly formed circles began appearing overnight in fields of
wheat, barley, and corn.

Initially, they were a local curiosity. Farmers would find them, locals would gossip, and the
occasional UFO enthusiast would take note. The early circles were not elaborate—just single circles
or simple arrangements. The prevailing theories at the time were:
*   **Unusual weather phenomena:** freak whirlwinds or lightning strikes.
*   **Animal activity:** deer or other animals chasing each other in circles.
*   **Extraterrestrial activity:** UFOs landing and leaving an imprint.

### 3. The Big Reveal: The Pranksters Doug and Dave

The phenomenon exploded in the late 1980s. The designs became more complex, the media coverage
became global, and a whole field of self-proclaimed experts ("cereologists") emerged to study them.
They claimed the stems of the plants were bent in a way that couldn't be replicated by mechanical
means and that strange energy readings were present inside the circles.

Then, in **1991**, the entire mystery was seemingly solved.

Two friends from Southampton, **Doug Bower and Dave Chorley**, came forward and confessed. They
announced to the press that they had been making the circles since 1976 as a prank.

**Their Story and Method:**
*   **Inspiration:** They were inspired by the "Tully Saucer Nests" story from Australia in 1966,
where a farmer claimed to see a UFO rise from a swamp, leaving behind a swirled circle of reeds.
*   **The Goal:** They wanted to make people think UFOs had landed.
*   **The Tools:** Their method was brilliantly simple. They used a **plank of wood, a rope, and a
baseball cap** fitted with a loop of wire for sighting to keep their lines straight. One person
would hold one end of the rope in the center, while the other walked in a circle with the other end
of the rope, pressing the crops down with the plank. This bent the stalks without breaking them,
just as the "experts" had described.
*   **Secrecy:** For over 15 years, they created hundreds of circles, often going out at night after
a pint at the pub. Not even their wives knew.

To prove their story, they took journalists out and created a perfect crop circle in broad daylight
in under an hour.

### 4. The Aftermath: The Prank Becomes an Art Form

You might think the story ends there, but it doesn't. The confession had an unexpected effect.
Instead of killing the phenomenon, **it inspired a new generation of "croppies" or "circle
makers."**

Doug and Dave's confession essentially gave away the secret and provided a "how-to" guide. The
simple prank evolved into a full-blown **land art movement**.

*   **Explosion of Complexity:** After 1991, the designs became exponentially more intricate.
Artists began incorporating complex mathematics, fractal geometry (like the Mandelbrot set), 3D
illusions, and references to ancient symbols and even astrophysics.
*   **Technology:** These new artists used computers, GPS, and laser pointers to plan and execute
their enormous and precise designs overnight. What started with a plank and a rope became a
sophisticated operation involving teams of people.

### 5. Why the Mystery Endures

Even with the confession and the clear evidence of human artistry, a dedicated community of
believers still holds that *some* circles are not man-made. Their arguments usually focus on:

*   **Bent Nodes:** They claim the nodes (knuckles) on the plant stems are bent or exploded in a way
that suggests intense, localized microwave radiation. Skeptics argue this is a natural result of the
plant being laid flat and then trying to right itself toward the sun (phototropism).
*   **Alleged Radiation:** Believers report strange energy fields, malfunctioning cameras, and
physical sensations inside "genuine" circles. These claims have never been substantiated under
controlled scientific conditions.
*   **Complexity and Speed:** Some argue that the most complex designs are impossible for humans to
create in the dark in a single night without leaving tracks. However, documentaries have repeatedly
shown teams of skilled circle makers doing exactly that.

### Conclusion: The Real Story

The overwhelming consensus, supported by direct evidence and confessions, is that crop circles are a
**human-created phenomenon**.

The story is not about aliens, but about human creativity and psychology. It began as a simple prank
by two friends, was amplified by media fascination and our collective love for a good mystery, and
ultimately blossomed into a unique and beautiful global art movement. It's a perfect example of how
a myth can be created, debunked, and yet continue to thrive in a new form.

shrouded in folklore and fueled by speculation. Here's a breakdown:

**The Core Phenomenon:**

*   **Definition:** Crop circles are patterns created by flattening crops, typically in fields of
wheat, barley, rye, or canola. They range from simple circles to intricate geometric designs, often
appearing overnight.

**Theories and Explanations:**

1.  **Hoaxes:**

    *   **The Most Accepted Explanation:** The vast majority of crop circles are now widely accepted
as hoaxes created by humans.
    *   **Doug and Dave:** In 1991, Doug Bower and Dave Chorley, two British artists, confessed to
creating hundreds of crop circles since the 1970s using simple tools like planks of wood, rope, and
wire. They demonstrated their techniques and proved that they could replicate many existing
formations.
    *   **Copycats and Continued Activity:** Following Doug and Dave's confession, the number and
complexity of crop circles increased significantly, suggesting that others were inspired or
emboldened to create their own.
    *   **Motivations:** The motivations for creating crop circles vary, ranging from artistic
expression and pranks to attracting attention, promoting tourism, or simply testing the limits of
belief.

2.  **Unexplained or Paranormal Theories (Less Credible):**

    *   **Extraterrestrial Origins:** Perhaps the most popular and enduring theory is that crop
circles are messages or signs left by extraterrestrial beings visiting Earth.
    *   **Vortexes or Energy Fields:** Some theories suggest that crop circles are formed by
localized vortexes of energy or unusual atmospheric phenomena.
    *   **Earth Energies:** Others propose that they are related to ley lines, earth energies, or
some form of terrestrial intelligence.
    *   **Military Experiments:** A few have speculated that the circles are accidental or
intentional results of military experiments involving advanced technology.

**Why the Appeal and Belief?**

*   **Visual Impact:** The sheer beauty and complexity of some crop circle formations are
undeniable, making them aesthetically appealing and thought-provoking.
*   **Mystery and Unexplained Elements:** Even with the prevalence of hoaxes, some formations are
very large and intricate, leading some to question whether they could be created so quickly and
accurately by human hands.
*   **Desire for the Extraordinary:** The belief in extraterrestrial life and the desire for
evidence of the unexplained are powerful drivers. Crop circles offer a tantalizing possibility of
something beyond our current understanding.
*   **Confirmation Bias:** People often seek out information that confirms their existing beliefs.
Those who already believe in the paranormal are more likely to accept theories about
extraterrestrial involvement.

**Important Considerations:**

*   **Lack of Physical Evidence:** No credible scientific evidence has ever been found to support
non-human origins for crop circles.
*   **Tool Marks and Human Activity:** Most circles exhibit signs of human creation, such as tool
marks, flattened crops that are broken rather than bent, and footprints.
*   **Geographic Concentration:** Crop circles tend to appear in areas where they are likely to
attract attention, such as near tourist attractions or in areas with a history of UFO sightings.
*   **Complexity Increase After Hoax Revelation:** The increase in complexity *after* Doug and
Dave's confession strongly suggests imitation and competition among hoaxers.

**In conclusion:** While the mystery of crop circles has captured the imagination of many, the
overwhelming evidence points to them being primarily the work of human artists and pranksters. While
the possibility of rare, unexplained natural phenomena cannot be completely dismissed, the
extraterrestrial or paranormal explanations lack credible scientific support. The story of crop
circles is less about alien visitors and more about human ingenuity, creativity, and the power of
belief.

"Crop circles have an interesting history that blends art, hoaxes, and mystery:\n\n## The
Phenomenon\n\nCrop circles are geometric patterns created by flattening crops, typically wheat or
barley. They range from simple circles to elaborate, complex designs that can span hundreds of
feet.\n\n## Timeline and Origins\n\n- **Pre-1970s**: Some reports of simple circles exist
historically, but they were rare and not well-documented\n- **1970s-1980s**: Circles began appearing
more frequently in southern England, particularly Wiltshire\n- **1990s**: Designs became
increasingly complex and spread worldwide\n\n## The Big Reveal\n\nIn 1991, two British men, Doug
Bower and Dave Chorley, confessed to creating hundreds of circles since 1978 using simple tools like
planks, rope, and wire. They demonstrated their techniques for the media, showing how intricate
patterns could be made in hours.\n\n## Current Understanding\n\nToday, crop circles are largely
understood to be:\n- **Human-made art**: Many circlemakers now openly create them as a form of
landscape art\n- **Tourist attractions**: Some farmers even commission them\n- **Ongoing pranks**:
Anonymous groups continue making them for fun\n\n## Why Some Remain \"Mysterious\"\n\n- No one
claims credit for every circle\n- Some appear overnight in remote locations\n- The precision of some
designs impresses observers\n- Various unsubstantiated theories persist (from wind vortices to
electromagnetic phenomena)\n\nWhile the vast majority are confirmed or presumed human-made, the

created by individuals or groups using simple tools like ropes and stakes. The most famous hoaxers,

and 1991.\n2. **Natural phenomena**: Some scientists have suggested that crop circles could be

circles.\n3. **Paranormal or extraterrestrial activity**: Some people believe that crop circles are
evidence of paranormal or extraterrestrial activity. This theory has been popularized by media and
folklore, with many claiming that the circles are messages or signs from otherworldly beings.\n4.
**Art and land art**: Some crop circles have been created as a form of land art or as a statement

MODEL OUTPUT:
"Short version: Almost all crop circles are made by people\u2014part prank, part land
art\u2014though a few simple patches of flattened crops come from wind and weather. There\u2019s no
credible evidence of a paranormal or extraterrestrial cause.\n\nThe story in a nutshell:\n- Early
mentions: People sometimes cite a 1678 \u201cMowing-Devil\u201d pamphlet, but that woodcut is a
morality tale, not evidence. A real antecedent was the 1966 \u201csaucer nest\u201d in Tully,
Australia\u2014likely wind on reeds.\n- Modern wave: The phenomenon took off in southern England in
the late 1970s and 1980s, especially in Wiltshire near ancient sites like Avebury and Stonehenge. At
first they were simple circles; then came rings, chains, and \u201cpictograms.\u201d\n- Hoaxers come
clean: In 1991, Doug Bower and Dave Chorley publicly showed how they\u2019d been making circles at
night since the mid\u20111970s using simple tools. After that, other teams and artists stepped in,
and the designs exploded in complexity (fractals, \u201cJulia sets,\u201d elaborate glyphs).\n- How
they\u2019re made: Circlemakers plan designs and lay them out with surveying techniques and simple
tools, often finishing large works in a few hours at night. Media demonstrations and time\u2011lapse
documentation have repeatedly shown this is feasible. (I won\u2019t provide step\u2011by\u2011step
instructions.)\n- Evidence it\u2019s human-made:\n  - Many groups have claimed and demonstrated
authorship; some formations are commissioned for ads.\n  - Tool marks, tramlines used as access
paths, and GPS-straight lines are common.\n  - People have been caught at night; some creators have
documented their work from start to finish.\n  - Plant and soil samples show mechanical flattening
consistent with boards/rollers. Claims of radiation, \u201cmicrowave\u201d effects, or unusual
growth nodes haven\u2019t stood up to controlled, peer-reviewed replication.\n- Alternative ideas:\n
- Atmospheric vortices (a meteorologist once proposed this for simple circles) can flatten patches
but don\u2019t explain complex geometry.\n  - Paranormal/ET claims persist in popular media, but no
reliable physical evidence supports them.\n- Why Wiltshire? Rolling cereal fields, lots of vantage
points for photography, proximity to iconic ancient sites (which adds mystique), and an active local
subculture of makers and enthusiasts.\n- Impact and culture: Crop circles draw tourists,
photographers, and festivals; they also damage farmers\u2019 crops and involve trespassing, so many
farmers mow them out quickly or charge entry to recoup losses.\n- Today: Formations still appear
worldwide (UK, Germany, Italy, Czech Republic, the US, Japan), though the peak craze was the
1990s\u20132000s. They\u2019re best understood as a mix of folk art, guerrilla performance, and
media spectacle.\n\nBottom line: The \u201cmystery\u201d is mostly social\u2014creativity, secrecy,
and storytelling\u2014rather than a scientific or extraterrestrial puzzle."
